## Behaviours captured in aged care needs assessments

This page presents behaviours captured in aged care needs assessments performed between July 2023 and June 2024 using the National Screening and Assessment Form:

Refer to Overview of Australia's aged care system for information on recent changes to Australia’s aged care system.

### Behaviours and symptoms recorded in comprehensive aged care needs assessments

The National Screening and Assessment Form was used for aged care needs assessments from 1 July 2015 until 30 June 2024. Comprehensive Assessments were used for people with complex and multiple care needs seeking higher levels of support. Compared with home support assessments, for people seeking entry-level community-based support, people undertaking comprehensive assessments were asked an expanded set of questions to better understand their care needs. Information on frequency of select behaviours that align with some behavioural and psychological symptoms of dementia (BPSD) was only captured in comprehensive aged care needs assessments.

For more information on aged care needs assessments, please visit Aged care needs assessments.

During a comprehensive aged care needs assessment using the National Screening and Assessment Form, people were asked how often (‘always’, ‘regularly’, ‘occasionally’ or ‘never’) they experience the following behaviours or symptoms:

- Agitation – described as extreme emotional disturbance.
- Anxiety – described as an unpleasant state of inner turmoil, often accompanied by nervous behaviours such as pacing back and forth, somatic complaints and rumination.
- Apathy – described as the absence or suppression of passion, emotion, or excitement.
- Depression – described as depressive symptoms, including physical symptoms, long periods of feeling lonely, overwhelming feelings of being unable to keep going or regular tears.
- Hallucinations or delusions – delusions are described as false or erroneous beliefs that usually involve a misinterpretation of perceptions or experiences. Hallucinations can occur in any sensory modality, including auditory, visual, olfactory (smell), gustatory (taste) and tactile (touch).
- Insomnia – described as persistent difficulty in initiating or maintaining sleep.
- Physical aggression – described as a person using physical force against others, objects or themselves.
- Risky behaviour – described as behaviours that put the person or others at risk of harm.
- Resistive behaviour – described as a person resisting, opposing or withstanding help or caregiving tasks such as taking medication, eating or self-feeding.
- Verbal aggression – described as a person yelling, screaming and/or threatening.
- Wandering – described as moving without definite destination or purpose.

In this report:

- People who reported experiencing any of these behaviours ‘always’ or ‘regularly’ were categorised as experiencing that symptom of BPSD.
- People who reported ‘occasionally’ or ‘never’ experiencing the symptom were categorised as not experiencing that symptom.
- People who did not answer the question when asked were categorised as ‘not stated’.
- People who did not answer all of the BPSD questions were excluded from these analyses.

As discussed in What are behavioural and psychological symptoms of dementia, the Neuropsychiatric Inventory (NPI) is the gold-standard assessment tool for identifying BPSD. It reports on the frequency, severity and carer distress associated with 12 domains, providing an overall picture of a person’s BPSD.

Although aged care needs assessments do not use the NPI, they record information on whether people experience select behaviours or symptoms that align, or partly align, with some BPSD domains in the NPI. Such information provides useful insight into the behaviours and symptoms experienced by people seeking access to aged care services. It also allows for comparisons between people with dementia and people who did not report having dementia.

However, these questions have not been validated for measuring BPSD, and the ‘symptoms’ recorded do not always reflect the preferred clinical terms. For example, the description of NPI domain *disinhibition* overlaps with the recorded symptom, *risky behaviours*, in aged care needs assessments. While we present information on people experiencing *risky behaviours* below as recorded in the data, *disinhibition *remains the preferred clinical term. Further, while *wandering* is not listed in the NPI, it is frequently cited as an example of BPSD in literature (RACGP 2019; Burley et al. 2021) and is listed as an example behaviour under the *Sleep and Nighttime Behaviours* NPI domain. As such, *wandering* information is included in this section.

For more details on the overlap of behaviours captured by the aged care needs assessment as compared to the gold-standard NPI tool, refer to Technical notes.

### Number of symptoms reported

Between July 2023 and June 2024, over 202,000 Australians completed a comprehensive aged care needs assessment and 15% (30,500) of people were recorded as having dementia. Among all people who completed a comprehensive aged care needs assessment, over half (51%, 106,000 people) reported experiencing one or more behavioural or psychological symptoms ‘regularly’ or ‘always.’

During their comprehensive aged care needs assessment, 3 in 5 people with dementia (60%, 18,300) and 1 in 2 people without dementia (51%, 87,700) reported experiencing one or more symptoms. People with dementia were nearly twice as likely to report experiencing three or more symptoms always or regularly (21%, 6,500) compared with people without dementia (11%, 18,800) (Figure 16.2; Table S16.7).

#### Figure 16.2: Proportion of people who completed a comprehensive aged care needs assessment between July 2023 and June 2024, by number of behavioural and psychological symptoms and dementia status

The bar chart shows the percentage of people with or without dementia by number of symptoms, ranging from zero symptoms, 1 to 2 symptoms, 3 or more symptoms.

Note: *Symptoms* group included people who reported experiencing at least one behavioural and psychological symptom of dementia as 'always' or 'regularly'. *No symptoms* group included people who reported 'occasionally' or 'never' or ‘not stated’ for all of the behavioural and psychological symptoms of dementia. People with all behavioural and psychological symptoms of dementia as ‘not stated’ were excluded from the analyses.

### Types of symptoms reported

Insomnia was the most frequently reported symptom among people who completed a comprehensive aged care needs assessment between July 2023 and June 2024 and rates were similar among people with dementia and people without dementia (31% and 36%, respectively). This was followed by anxiety (22% and 20%, respectively) and depression (16% and 17%, respectively) (Table S16.8).

The other 8 BPSD symptoms measured in comprehensive aged care needs assessments were more commonly reported by people with dementia compared with people without dementia. The following symptoms had the greatest percentage point difference:

- Resistive behaviours – 15% of people with dementia compared with 3.1% of people without dementia.
- Agitation – 12% of people with dementia compared with 3.1% of people without dementia.
- Risky behaviours – 12% of people with dementia compared with 2.5% of people without dementia.
- Wandering – 8% of people with dementia compared with 0.6% of people without dementia (Figure 16.3a).

While rare, physical aggression was reported more frequently by people with dementia (1.2%) compared with people without dementia (0.1%).

It is important to note that while frequency of symptoms is captured within the comprehensive aged care needs assessment data, there is no indication of symptom severity or carer distress. Data in this section are based on an individual reporting their experience of a particular symptom to be ‘always’ or ‘regularly,’ however it is difficult to estimate the full extent of symptom impact without further severity and distress information.

#### Figure 16.3a: Proportion of people who reported experiencing behavioural and psychological symptoms ‘always’ or ‘regularly’ in comprehensive aged care needs assessment between July 2023 and June 2024, by symptom type

The bar chart shows the percentage of people with or without dementia by type of behaviour and psychological symptom.

Note: People with all behavioural and psychological symptoms of dementia as ‘not stated’ were excluded from the analyses.

**Symptoms decreased with age and varied by sex**

Among people who completed a comprehensive aged care needs assessment and had dementia recorded as a health condition:

- A higher proportion of men reported experiencing symptoms of agitation, apathy, insomnia, risky behaviour, verbal aggression and wandering ‘always’ or ‘regularly’ compared with women. A higher proportion of women reported experiencing symptoms of anxiety, depression and resistive behaviours ‘always’ or ‘regularly’ compared with men.
- The proportion of people with dementia reporting all symptom types decreased with age, except for insomnia and physical aggression, which increased in the 65–74 age group and then decreased. For people without dementia, the proportion of people reporting all symptoms types typically decreased with age (Figure 16.3b; Table S16.9).

These patterns were similar for people who did not report having dementia, except that insomnia was more commonly reported by women, resistive behaviours were more commonly reported by men, and reporting of all symptoms tended to decrease with age.

Within Figure 16.3b, the proportion of the ‘not stated’ responses are presented for each symptom. Across all symptom types, there were a greater proportion of ‘not stated’ responses for people who recorded dementia as a health condition compared to people who did not (except for those aged below 65 with symptoms of risky behaviour). As such, it is likely that these estimates disproportionately undercount the frequency of symptoms experienced among people with dementia who completed a comprehensive aged care needs assessment.

#### Figure 16.3b: Reported symptoms among people who completed a comprehensive aged care needs assessment between July 2023 and June 2024, by symptom type, age and sex

The bar chart shows the percentage of people with or without dementia by symptom type, 10-year age and sex. Dropdown buttons allow the option to view the data by age or sex, by symptom type.

Note: People who reported experiencing that particular behavioural and psychological symptom of dementia as 'always' or 'regularly' were classified as *Symptoms* group, 'occasionally' or 'never' were classified as *No symptoms* group, and ‘not stated’ were classified as *Not stated* group. People with all behavioural and psychological symptoms of dementia as ‘not stated’ were excluded from the analyses.

### Triggers for assessment

Comprehensive aged care needs assessments using the National Screening and Assessment Form recorded the triggers that indicated the need for an individual’s assessment to take place. These triggers may be closely related to an individual’s health and care needs, or related to other circumstances such as a change in care arrangements. An individual may also have had more than one trigger for assessment recorded.

Between July 2023 and June 2024, most comprehensive aged care needs assessments for people with dementia who reported behavioural or psychological symptoms were triggered by:

- a change in cognitive status (88%)
- a change in care needs (83%)
- a change in medical condition (79%).

These triggers were also common among people with dementia who did not report symptoms (84%, 80% and 78%, respectively). For every trigger, there was a higher or similar proportion of people with symptoms compared with people without symptoms (Table S16.10).

Burley C, Casey AN, Chenoweth L and Brodaty H (2021) ‘Reconceptualising Behavioural and Psychological Symptoms of Dementia: Views of People Living With Dementia and Families/Care Partners’, *Frontiers in Psychiatry, *doi: doi.org/10.3389/fpsyt.2021.710703.

RACGP (Royal Australian College of General Practitioner) (2019) *RACGP aged care clinical guide (Silver Book)**, *RACGP, accessed 11 November 2022.

## Data gaps and limitations

Despite the high impact of behavioural and psychological symptoms of dementia (BPSD) on quality of life and care needs, there are currently no national prevalence estimates of BPSD among people with dementia in Australia.

While the primary Australian service planning model adopted in 2003 estimates that approximately 60% of people with dementia experience BPSD, estimates across the literature fluctuate depending on sample characteristics (community-based sample compared with residential aged care or hospital based) and measurement methodology (Brodaty, Draper and Low 2003).

The estimates most recently reported by Australian studies range from 61% to 88% of people with dementia in the community setting, 29% to 90% of residents in Australian nursing homes and 95% of hospitalised patients in long term acute care (APH 2014). However, these estimates of BPSD prevalence are based on studies with smaller samples conducted between 1996 and 2001, highlighting the need for studies with more robust and representative samples and up-to-date prevalence estimates. In addition, while prevalence estimates indicate an increase in dementia over the next decade, there has been no research that confirms whether rates of BPSD will similarly rise (NSW Health 2021). A more recent international meta-analysis study of articles published during the period 1997 to 2024 revealed a pooled prevalence of overall BPSD (one or more BPSD symptoms) at 60% among older inpatients with dementia in acute hospitals (Kanthee A et. al 2025).

Despite its importance and impact, there are key gaps in our understanding of BPSD in Australia. Firstly, there is no singular cause of BPSD. Causes and influences vary between individuals, ranging from physiological changes in the brain due to dementia, to unmet needs or physical co-morbidities. There are also significant difficulties in untangling separate mental health conditions from BPSD and dementia during disease progression. Similarly, there is no diagnostic code for BPSD in available administrative data and often behaviour concerns are not distinguished from a dementia diagnosis in data. Understanding the drivers and influences of BPSD is key in developing targeted treatment and support services.

Further, while there are a range of management and support services available for people experiencing BPSD, synthesising data and patterns from service use and management techniques is a key challenge. Pharmacological treatments are difficult to estimate due to the range of medications that can be used to manage BPSD, including off-label prescriptions. Similarly, non-pharmacological treatment services range from BPSD-specific programs to broader exercise and counselling services. Greater identification of BPSD is needed in administrative datasets to understand the health and aged care service use patterns among people experiencing BPSD in Australia. The AIHW are currently exploring opportunities through our linked data in the National Health Data Hub to provide a more comprehensive picture across all tiers of BPSD in Australia, including geographical and residential location, demographic background and utilisation of support services.

APH (Australian Parliament House) (2014) *Care and management of younger and older Australians living with dementia and behavioural and psychiatric symptoms of dementia (BPSD)*, Canberra: APH, accessed 9 December 2021.

Brodaty H, Draper B and Low LF (2003) ‘Behavioural and psychological symptoms of dementia: a seven-tiered model of service delivery’, *The Medical Journal of Australia *178(5): 231-234, doi:10.5694/j.1326-5377.2003.tb05169.x.

Kanthee A, Aimorn J, Warut A, Katti S, Nisan W, Teerapat T, Pakawat W, Nathan D, Elizabeth L S (2025), ‘Behavioural and psychological symptoms of people with dementia in acute hospital settings: a systematic review and meta-analysis’, *Age and Ageing* 54 (1), doi: 10.1093/ageing/afaf013

NSW Health (New South Wales Ministry of Health) (2021) *Extreme Behavioural and Psychological Symptoms of Dementia (BPSD*, NSW Health, accessed 17 January 2022.

## Spending on dementia

The following pages present the estimated direct expenditure in 2023–24 that is attributable to the additional cost of caring for people with dementia, including:

- Total health and aged care spending on dementia
- Spending on aged care and support services
- Spending on health care

Australia’s response to dementia requires economic investment across the health, aged care and welfare sectors. This investment includes spending associated with diagnosis, treatment and care of people with dementia and support services for people with dementia and their informal carers.

Refer to the Technical notes for detailed information on the methodology and data sources used to estimate expenditure directly attributable to dementia. The available data and key limitations are also summarised below.

Refer to the Spending on dementia data tables for the underlying data presented in these pages.

### Key statistics

This report focuses on health and aged care expenditure estimates that:

- are
**directly attributable to dementia**(rather than all costs for people with dementia), to avoid including expenditure related to other conditions - have been adjusted to
**reflect the contribution of comorbidities**to the costs for treatment and care of people with dementia **excludes indirect expenditure**, such as costs arising from the social and economic burden on carers and family, or from lost wages and productivity.

Most spending data in this report are presented in ‘current prices’, meaning they reflect the dollar amount spent in that year without adjusting for inflation.

Detailed information on the methodology and data sources used to estimate expenditure directly attributable to dementia can be found in the Technical notes, for the following areas of spending:

#### Aged care

This section previously used health condition data from the Aged Care Funding Instrument to identify people with dementia in permanent residential aged care.

In October 2022 the Aged Care Funding Instrument was replaced with the Australian National Aged Care Classification funding model, which does not capture health condition information.

In the absence of updated funding data for dementia in residential aged care, the proportion of spending allocated to dementia in residential aged care from the Aged Care Funding Instrument 2020–21 was applied to 2023–24 residential aged care spending data.

The AIHW is working with the Department of Health, Disability and Ageing to determine appropriate methods to capture data on people living with dementia in aged care.

- Australian Government expenditure on aged care programs and assessments is sourced from publicly available reports (Department of Health, Disability and Ageing 2024, Productivity Commission 2025). This is combined with the proportion of people with dementia accessing aged care programs and assessments (derived using the National Aged Care Data Clearinghouse) to estimate expenditure on aged care programs due to dementia.
- Data from the National Screening and Assessment Form for 2023–24 were used to allocate spending on the Commonwealth Home Support Program, respite care services, Transition Care Program, Veterans Home Care Program and aged care needs assessments due to dementia.
- Data from the Home Care Packages Program for 2023–24 were used to allocate spending on the Home Care Packages Program and Department of Veterans’ Affairs Community Nursing Program.
- Proportional spending due to dementia from the 2020–21 Aged Care Funding Instrument, together with the 2018 Survey of Disability Ageing and Carers, was used to estimate spending on permanent residential aged care due to dementia in 2023–24.
- For National Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Flexible Aged Care Program a hybrid approach was used, incorporating data from the 2023–24 National Screening and Assessment Form and 2020–21 Aged Care Funding Instrument. For more information, refer to the Technical notes.

Refer to Aged care needs assessments and Community-based aged care for information on recent changes to Australia’s aged care system.

#### Dementia Support Services

Direct spending on dementia-specific programs, packages and services in 2023–24 is sourced from the Australian Government Department of Health, Disability and Ageing.

#### Health care

Health care expenditure attributable to dementia in 2023–24 is derived from the AIHW’s Disease Expenditure Database, that contains spending estimates for 17 Australian Burden of Disease Study (ABDS) groups and the 220 conditions within those groups (AIHW 2025). Expenditure is reported by area of expenditure, age group and sex. The data in the AIHW Disease Expenditure Database includes all sources of funding, including government and non-government expenditure (such as patient co-payments). Spending estimates are based on hospital admissions, emergency department records, outpatient records, MBS and PBS records.

Changes in health care spending between 2020–01 and 2023–24 are presented in both ‘current prices’, reflecting the actual amount spent in each year, as well as ‘constant prices’, which account for the impact of inflation and changes in the value of money. Constant price expenditure for all years is expressed in 2023–24 prices.

There is no single source of data to report total expenditure due to dementia, and several different data sources of varying quality are used to estimate the costs of dementia.

It should be noted that current expenditure estimates are conservative due to data limitations, as it excludes costs such as state and territory expenditure on aged care, government payments to support people with dementia and their carers, and indirect expenditure (such as lost wages and productivity).

To estimate spending on dementia in residential aged care, dementia data from the Aged Care Funding Instrument 2020–21 was applied to total spending in 2023–24. This may be an underestimate as the number of people with dementia in residential aged care and the proportion of spending on people with dementia in these settings are unlikely to have decreased in the 2-year period.

There are ongoing efforts to improve the accuracy of dementia costs estimates in Australia. See *14: Direct and indirect costs of dementia to the Australian economy* of the National Dementia Data Improvement Plan 2023–2034 for information on current developments and future activities aimed at improving dementia cost data.

AIHW (Australian Institute of Health and Welfare) (2025) *Health system spending on disease and injury in Australia, 2023–24*, AIHW, Australian Government, accessed 29 October 2025.

Department of Health, Disability and Ageing (2024) *2023–24 Report on the Operation of the Aged Care Act 1997*, Department of Health, Disability and Ageing, Australian Government, accessed 23 March 2026.

Productivity Commission (2025) *Report on Government Services 2025*, Productivity Commission, Australian Government, accessed 23 March 2026.

## Total health and aged care spending on dementia

In 2023–24, an estimated $6 billion was spent on aged care, health care and dementia support programs for the care, diagnosis and treatment of people with dementia.

Of the estimated $6 billion spent on dementia:

- over two-thirds (70%) was spent on aged care ($4.2 billion),
- over one quarter (27%) was spent on health care ($1.6 billion) and
- 2.5% was spent on dementia support services and programs ($151 million).

The largest area of spending was for permanent residential aged care ($2.7 billion), followed by hospital services ($1.4 billion, including $1.0 billion for admitted patient care in public hospitals) and community-based aged care services ($1.2 billion, including $1.0 billion for the Home Care Packages Program) (Figure 17.1).

#### Figure 17.1: Health and aged care system spending attributable to dementia in 2023–24: percentage by broad area

Bar graph showing the distribution of dementia expenditure between broad service areas. It shows that the largest area of spending was on residential aged care services.

Note: See Technical notes for sources and methods used to derive estimates for each of the service areas/programs.

## Spending on aged care and support services

This page presents the estimated expenditure on aged care services and assessments for people with dementia as well as Australian Government spending on dementia-specific support services in 2023–24.

### Key statistics

**$4.2 billion**was spent on aged care services and assessments directly for dementia

**64%**($2.7 billion) of spending on aged care for dementia was for permanent residential aged care

**28% (nearly $1.2 billion)**of spending on aged care for dementia was for community-based aged care

**$151 million**to fund dementia support programs and initiatives

### Aged care expenditure

There are a range of government-funded aged care services available to provide care and assistance to those who need it, including: home-based care and support, short-term care and respite, and residential aged care services.

Compared to 2022–23, the total spending on aged care for people with dementia in 2023–24 has increased by over $1 billion, in line with overall increase in spending on aged care.

From June 2023, the Australian Government committed to $11.3 billion over 4 years to support the Fair Work Commission’s interim decision to increase the minimum award wage for direct aged care workers by 15% (Department of Health, Disability and Ageing, 2026). Further award wage increases for these and other aged care workers were implemented from January 2025 onwards.

This period also includes a transition in funding models for aged care, with part of 2022–23 funded under the Aged Care Funding Instrument (ACFI) and 2023–24 fully funded under the Australian National Aged Care Classification (AN‑ACC).

In 2023–24 the Australian Government spent over $33 billion on aged care services and assessments, and $4.2 billion (13%) of this was estimated to be spent on people with dementia (Table S17.2). Of the estimated $4.2 billion spent on aged care services and assessments for dementia:

- 64% ($2.7 billion) was for permanent residential aged care
- 28% ($1.2 billion) was for community-based aged care services
- 5.4% ($228 million) was for respite care services
- 1.9% ($78 million) was for flexible aged care services
- 0.5% ($22 million) was for aged care needs assessments (Table S17.1; Figure 17.2).

The proportion of spending on aged care services and assessments due to dementia ranged from 3.7% for the Commonwealth Home Support Program to 23% for community-based respite care (Table S17.2).

Estimated spending on community-based aged care for dementia included:

- $1 billion for people with dementia receiving Home Care Packages – services that provide support for people with multiple or complex care needs to live independently in their own homes. More than 90% of dementia‑attributable HCP spending is concentrated in Level 3 and 4 packages, the highest funding levels (Table 17.3). This includes $110 million spent on the dementia and cognition supplement. Of this, 61% was for Level 4 packages, which are designed for people with high care needs (Table S17.3).
- $110 million for people with dementia under the Commonwealth Home Support Program – a program that provides entry level care for people who require a low level of support to remain living independently and safely at home.
- $17 million for people with dementia receiving the Veterans Home Care program – a program that provides domestic assistance, personal care, and respite care to support veterans and their families to live independently in their own home.
- $9.6 million for people with dementia receiving the Department of Veterans' Affairs Community Nursing program – which provides clinically required nursing and personal care for veterans and their families in their own home.

Estimated spending on respite care services for dementia included:

- $153 million for people with dementia receiving residential respite care – short-term care delivered in residential aged care homes
- $75 million for people with dementia receiving community-based respite care, short-term care delivered in the person’s home, centre-based day respite and overnight or weekend cottage respite

Estimated spending on flexible aged care services for dementia included:

- $41 million for people with dementia receiving the National Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Flexible Aged Care Program – which provides culturally appropriate aged care at home and in the community for First Nations people.
- $37 million for people with dementia receiving the Transition Care Programme – which provides short-term care to restore independent living after a hospital stay.

For more information on aged care services for people with dementia, refer to Aged care needs assessments and Community-based aged care.

#### Figure 17.2: Estimated Australian Government aged care spending due to dementia in 2023–24

Bar graph showing Australian Government expenditure on different aged care programs in 2023–24 that were directly attributable to dementia.

Note: Expenditure for residential aged care services is for permanent residents only.

### Expenditure on dementia-specific support services

This section reports on select dementia support programs and initiatives funded by the Australian Government and delivered by individual organisations. These programs are designed to assist people with dementia and their carers, as well as the dementia care workforce.

In 2023–24, the Australian Government provided over $151 million to fund dementia support programs and initiatives under the Dementia and Aged Care Services Fund (Table S17.1). This included:

- $31 million for the Specialist Dementia Care Program
- $27 million for the National Dementia Training Program – a national approach to accredited education, upskilling and professional development in dementia care delivered by Dementia Training Australia
- $26 million for the Dementia Behavioural Management Advisory Service
- $25 million for the National Dementia Support Program
- $21 million for Severe Behaviour Response Teams
- $20 million to improve respite care for people living with dementia and their carers
- $2 million for Dementia-Friendly Communities, a program to build greater awareness, acceptance and understanding of dementia in the community.

For further information on the Dementia and Aged Care Services Fund and provision of dementia support services in recent years, refer to Overview of dementia support services and initiatives, National policy response to dementia and National dementia behaviour support programs.

Department of Health, Disability and Ageing (2026) *Better and fairer wages for aged care workers*. Australian Government Department of Health, Disability and Ageing, accessed 12 March 2026.

## Spending on health care

This page presents the total health expenditure directly attributable to the cost of caring for people with dementia in 2023–24, including:

#### Key statistics

**$1.6 billion**was spent on health care directly for dementia

**$1 billion**was spent on public hospital admitted patient care and

**$33 million**on private hospital admitted patient care

**$565 million**($435 million without inflation) from 2020–21 to 2023–24

**2 times**higher for patients with dementia

### Total health care spending on dementia

In 2023–24, it was estimated that over $1.6 billion was spent on health care due to dementia. Most of this expenditure (88%, over $1.4 billion) was for hospital-related services, which included:

- $1.0 billion for public hospital admitted patient care and $33 million for private hospital admitted patient care
- $261 million for outpatient clinics in public hospitals
- $88 million for emergency department care in public hospitals

Primary health care services and referred medical services each contributed to around 6% of spending on health care due to dementia in 2023–24 (Figure 17.3; Table S17.1).

Note that, as with other estimates in this report, spending on public hospital admitted patient care only includes dementia-specific costs of hospital separations (referred to as ‘hospitalisations’) where dementia was recorded as a principal or additional diagnosis. This means that the cost of managing other conditions in the same hospitalisation are not included, regardless of whether dementia was the principal or additional diagnosis in the hospitalisation.

#### Figure 17.3: Estimated health-care system expenditure attributable to dementia in 2023–24: by health-care area

Bar graph showing the health-care system expenditure attributable to dementia in 2023–24, by health-care area. The graph shows that most of the expenditure was on public hospital admitted patient care and public hospital outpatient clinic services. This was followed by spending on public hospital emergency department care, private hospital admitted patient care, general practitioner services, medical imaging, specialist services and Pharmaceutical Benefit Scheme.

### Changes in health care spending over time

In this section, changes in spending are presented in ‘constant prices’ which account for the impact of inflation and changes in the value of money. In contrast, ‘current prices’ reflect actual spending in each year.

#### Figure 17.4: Changes in health care expenditure attributable to dementia between 2020–21 and 2023–24, current and constant prices

| Year | Dementia spending (constant prices) | Dementia spending (current prices) |
|---|---|---|
| 2020–21 | 1.19 billion | 1.06 billion |
| 2021–22 | 1.35 billion | 1.23 billion |
| 2022–23 | 1.54 billion | 1.46 billion |
| 2023–24 | 1.63 billion | 1.63 billion |

Notes:

1. Constant price health spending is in 2023–24 prices.

2. Nominal changes in spending from year to year refer to the change in current price estimates. Real growth is the growth in spending at constant prices.

3. For details of the methods used to produce the disease expenditure estimates, please see: Health system spending on disease and injury in Australia 2023–24: Overview of analysis and methodology report.

Source:
AIHW Disease Expenditure Database


Using the latest AIHW Disease Expenditure Database methodology (AIHW 2025a), the estimated spending on health care due to dementia in constant prices (adjusted for inflation) was:

- $1.2 billion in 2020–21
- $1.3 billion in 2021–22
- $1.5 billion in 2022–23
- $1.6 billion in 2023–24

Estimated annual real growth (measured in constant prices) in spending on health care due to dementia was 13% between 2020–21 and 2021–22, 14% between 2021–22 and 2022–23 and 6% between 2022–23 and 2023–24 (Table S17.4a).

Over the same period, total spending on disease and injury in constant prices was:

- $168 billion in 2020–21
- $173 billion in 2021–22
- $179 billion in 2022–23
- $180 billion in 2023–24

Estimated annual real growth in total spending on disease and injury was 3.1% between 2020–21 and 2021–22, 3.4% between 2021–22 and 2022–23 and 0.9% between 2022–23 and 2023–24 (Table S17.4b).

Though total spending on disease and injury is much larger than the dementia specific expenditure, the growth in expenditure due to dementia is higher than growth in total spending on disease and injury. Further work is needed to understand the drivers behind this growth, which may include factors such as the ageing population contributing to higher numbers of people with dementia or may indicate changing patterns in the use of services.

### Hospital services expenditure

#### Admitted patient care in public hospitals

In 2023–24, over $1 billion was spent on public hospital admitted patient care for dementia, 64% of the total direct health care cost of dementia.

Of this, over $426 million (nearly 41%) was for hospitalisations where dementia was the principal diagnosis (or the main reason for admission). The remaining $619 million (or 59%) was for hospitalisations where dementia was an additional diagnosis – where dementia impacted care but was not the main reason for being admitted to hospital (Table S17.5).

Spending on public hospital admitted patient care due to dementia was higher for men ($547 million) compared with women ($498 million) and varied by age. For men and women the average cost per hospitalisation was highest in those aged under 65 and decreased with age, ranging from around $35,500 for men and around $41,200 for women aged under 65 to around $24,700 for men and around $23,700 for women aged 85 and over (Table S17.6). This reflects the age and sex patterns observed in hospitalisations due to dementia in 2023–24. For more information refer to Hospitalisations due to dementia. In 2023–24 over $33 million was spent on private hospital admitted patient care for dementia, 2% of the total direct health care cost of dementia (Table S17.1).

There were fewer admissions due to dementia in private hospitals (around 3,600) compared with public hospitals (around 25,100). Patients admitted to private hospitals tended to have fewer co-morbidities, indicating less complex care needs, compared with those admitted to public hospitals. The average cost per hospitalisation for patients with dementia was around $9,200 in private hospitals and around $27,800 in public hospitals. Unlike trends observed in public hospitals, the average cost per hospitalisation in private hospitals was highest in those aged 85 and over and tended to decrease with age – ranging from $13,000 for men and $10,100 for women aged 85 and over to $3,300 for men and $4,100 for women aged under 65 (Table S17.6).

These estimates are for expenditure directly related to dementia and not for the management of other conditions in the same hospitalisation. For more information on these estimates by age group and sex, refer to the Spending on dementia data tables.

#### How does dementia affect the cost of hospitalisations in public hospitals for common principal diagnoses?

A diagnosis of dementia will influence the cost of a hospitalisation even when it is not the main reason for being admitted to hospital.

To understand the costs related to dementia patients in hospital, the average costs of hospitalisations in a public hospital with an additional diagnosis of dementia were compared to those without dementia for common principal diagnoses seen among older people (Table S17.7). Total spending on patients with dementia hospitalised for these diagnoses is also presented, providing insights into conditions that are both common and costly (Table S17.8). The average cost per hospitalisation was generally greater among patients with dementia than patients without dementia for common diagnoses, reflecting greater clinical complexity or longer lengths of stay in hospital (Table S17.7).

Abnormalities of gait and mobility had the greatest difference in the average cost per hospitalisation. It typically cost 2 times higher to care for patients with dementia hospitalised due to this condition ($20,800) compared to those without dementia ($10,300). In total, $7.9 million was spent on caring for patients with dementia hospitalised due to abnormalities of gait and mobility, and over half (53%) of these costs were attributable to dementia ($4.2 million, Table S17.8).

Fracture of femur had the greatest total spending of the diagnoses considered (Table S17.8). Nearly 20% of total spending on patients hospitalised with dementia was for fracture of femur ($118 million), with 18% of these costs attributable to dementia ($21.6 million). The average cost of hospitalisations due to fracture of femur was 1.2 times higher for patients with dementia ($33,800) compared with patients without dementia ($28,300) (Table S17.7).

In some cases, the average cost per hospitalisation was lower for patients living with dementia – including for people hospitalised due to sepsis (0.7 times or 30% less) or pneumonitis due to solids and liquids (0.9 times or 10% less). People with dementia who were hospitalised due to these conditions in 2023–24 had a shorter average length of stay compared to people without dementia, and were more likely to die during their hospitalisation. For both conditions, people with dementia were more likely to be admitted from and returning to a residential aged care home, which may also impact time spent in hospital.

### Public hospital outpatient clinics and emergency department care expenditure

In 2023–24, $261 million was spent on non-admitted patient care for dementia in public hospital outpatient clinics, equivalent to 16% of the total direct health care spending attributable to dementia (Table S17.1). Services provided in outpatient clinics include: consultations with specialist medical practitioners; allied health and specialist nursing care; diagnostic and other procedures; dispensing of medicines; and some community health services provided by hospitals (AIHW 2025b).

A further $88 million was spent on emergency department care in public hospitals, equivalent to 5.4% of the total direct health spending due to dementia (Table S17.1). For more information on health condition coding in emergency department data, refer to the Technical notes.

For more information on average cost per public hospital outpatient clinic or public hospital emergency department care by age group and sex, refer to the Spending on dementia data tables.

### Expenditure on primary health care and referred medical services

Spending on primary health care and referred medical services is equivalent to 12% of the total direct health expenditure on dementia (over $199 million, Table S17.1). These estimates are for services where dementia was directly managed and do not include services for patients with dementia where management and treatment related only to their other co-existing health conditions.

Services contributing to this expenditure were:

- GP services – almost $67 million
- medical imaging – almost $55 million
- specialist services – almost $28 million
- pharmaceuticals dispensed under the Pharmaceutical Benefits Scheme (PBS) – over $27 million
- this includes dementia-specific medicines (almost $24 million) and a range of other medications prescribed for the management of dementia symptoms

- pathology services – almost $12 million
- allied health and other services – over $10 million.

Spending on all primary health care and referred medical services for dementia was higher for women compared with men, ranging from 68% of GP services (over $45 million) to 58% of pharmaceuticals dispensed under the PBS (almost $16 million) spent on women. Almost two thirds of the $105 million spent on primary health care and $95 million spent on referred medical services was spent on women (Table S17.11).

The estimates of expenditure on primary health and referred medical services include Australian Government costs (under the Medicare Benefits Schedule (MBS) and the PBS), as well as out-of-pocket costs by individuals. The majority of the spending on primary health and referred medical services for dementia (89%, or just under $178 million) was paid for by the Australian Government (Table S17.12).

### Expenditure on medications for dementia

There are 4 dementia-specific medications – Donepezil, Galantamine, Rivastigmine and Memantine – currently subsidised by the Australian Government through the PBS and the Repatriation Pharmaceutical Benefits Scheme (RPBS) for treatment of Alzheimer’s disease. For information on the dispensing of scripts for these medications in Australia in recent years, see Prescriptions for dementia-specific medications.

In 2023–24, the total expenditure on dementia-specific medications was almost $24 million (Table S17.13). This included Australian Government expenditure (over $18 million, or 78% of expenditure on dementia-specific medications) and out-of-pocket expenses by patients (over $5 million or 22%). Note, these estimates may include a small amount of expenditure for off-label prescribing of these medications which are not captured in the PBS or RPBS.

Of the total expenditure for dementia-specific medications, nearly half was on Donepezil (42% or $10 million), followed by Rivastigmine (29% or $6.8 million), Memantine (18% or $4.4 million) and Galantamine (11% or $2.7 million).

The Australian Government subsidised most of the expenditure on each type of dementia-specific medication (Figure 17.5; Table S17.13). For patients, the average out-of-pocket cost for a single prescription for a dementia-specific medication was about $7. The average government-subsidised amount per prescription varied substantially - where the cost of Rivastigmine ($71) was over twice as much as Memantine ($35) and Galantamine ($32) and over 5 times as much as Donepezil ($14).

#### Figure 17.5: Dementia-specific medications expenditure by medication type in 2023–24: average price per script by source of expenditure

Bar graph showing expenditure on four dementia-specific medications (Rivastigmine, Memantine, Galantamine and Donepezil) in 2023–24. The graph shows average cost per script for each medication, by source of expenditure.

AIHW (Australian Institute of Health and Welfare) (2025a) *Health system spending on disease and injury in Australia, 2023–24,* AIHW, Australian Government, accessed 29 October 2025

AIHW (2025b) *Non-admitted patient clinic types*, AIHW, Australian Government, accessed on 24 March 2026.

## National policy response to dementia

### On this page In this section

Introduction The Royal Commission into Aged Care Quality and Safety The Australian Government’s response to the Royal Commission into Aged Care Quality and Safety The National Dementia Action Plan The Aged Care Diversity Framework Dementia research The AIHW National Centre for Monitoring DementiaAustralia was one of the first countries to design comprehensive dementia-specific policy initiatives at a national level, in response to the rising challenges of an expanding and ageing population and advocacy from groups, such as the then Alzheimer’s Association of Australia (Hunter and Doyle 2014).

The first federal dementia policy initiative was launched in 1992 (the National Plan for Dementia Care 1992–1997) and succeeding policy initiatives have cemented dementia as a key priority in the national aged care agenda. Dementia has also been increasingly recognised as a national health priority requiring a focus on preventive strategies, high-quality health and social care, and investments in medical research (Hunter and Doyle 2014; Department of Health, Disability and Ageing 2025a). In 2017, the World Health Organization developed The Global action plan on the public health response to dementia 2017–2025 to encourage a concerted international effort to address the global impacts of dementia. The global action plan was adopted by Australia and other Member States (WHO 2017).

This webpage focuses on:

- the Royal Commission into Aged Care Quality and Safety, including dementia-focused recommendations and the Australian Government’s response to these recommendations.
- the National Dementia Action Plan
- the Aged Care Diversity Framework
- government-funded dementia research
- the AIHW National Centre for Monitoring Dementia.

While this page focuses on the national policy response to dementia by the Australian Government, it is acknowledged that governments at all levels develop and deliver dementia-specific policies and services. State and territory governments also fund (in full or part) essential services such as: memory clinics; geriatric evaluations and aged care visiting services; older adult mental health services; hospital to residential aged care transition services; and support for people experiencing behaviours and psychological symptoms of dementia (Mond 2019).

Some of these essential services also receive Australian Government funding, for example through the Medicare Benefits Schedule or through specific programs and initiatives (for example, the Dementia Behaviour Management Advisory Service, Severe Behaviour Response Teams and the Specialist Dementia Care Program).

Recent Aged Care funding initiatives can be found on the Department of Health, Disability and Ageing website.

### The Royal Commission into Aged Care Quality and Safety

The Royal Commission into Aged Care Quality and Safety (Royal Commission) was set up in 2018 to look into issues related to the quality of residential and in-home aged care. The Royal Commission’s final report, Care, Dignity and Respect, was tabled in the Australian Parliament on 1 March 2021. This report included 148 wide-ranging recommendations for fundamental reform of the aged care system.

The Royal Commission identified four areas in need of immediate attention: food and nutrition; care and support for people living with dementia; elimination and reduction of restrictive practices; and palliative care (Royal Commission 2021).

Recommendations specific to dementia from the final report are:

**Recommendation 15:**establishing a comprehensive, clear and**accessible post-diagnosis support pathway**for people living with dementia, their carers and families.**Recommendation 16:**reviewing and publicly reporting on whether the number of**Specialist Dementia Care Units**established or planned to be established is sufficient to address need within the areas and populations they are designed to cover.**Recommendation 19:**reviewing of the Aged Care Quality Standards, including**dementia care standards.****Recommendation 45:**improving the design of aged care accommodation, including**dementia-friendly design.****Recommendation 80:**implementing**mandatory dementia care training**for workers engaged in residential aged care and in care at home (Royal Commission 2021).

### The Australian Government’s response to the Royal Commission into Aged Care Quality and Safety

A range of changes have been made to aged care in response to the final report of the Royal Commission. These changes are delivering significant reform in aged care, providing care, dignity and respect to older people.

The main response to these recommendations, was the *Aged Care Act 2024*. The new Act started on 1 November 2025. For more information see Overview of Australia’s aged care system.

The strengthened Aged Care Quality Standards (strengthened Quality Standards) are part of the new *Aged Care Act 2024* and have been in place since 1 November 2025 (Aged Care Quality and Safety Commission 2025; Department of Health, Disability and Ageing 2025b). They are a set of requirements for what quality and safe aged care looks like.

The strengthened Quality Standards make sure that the care older people receive:

- is safe and high quality
- meets their needs and preferences
- upholds their rights.

The strengthened Quality Standards have dementia and diversity embedded in them and significantly enhance expectations for the delivery of safe and quality care. They are more detailed and measurable than the previous Quality Standards, which were in place before the start of the new Act.

Dementia-specific changes will support people living with dementia and their carers at every stage from pre-diagnosis through to residential aged care. Together this will ensure people living with dementia are connected with the support they need, are enabled to remain in their home for longer, and able to access high quality, dementia informed aged care services.

Key initiatives include:

- improved early intervention assistance
- more help to navigate the aged care system
- more staff trained in dementia care
- improved respite experience for people living with dementia
- improvements in capacity and quality of dementia care
- more support for carers to maintain their caring role.

For more information on key dementia initiatives see Our work related to dementia | Australian Government Department of Health, Disability and Ageing.

The Australian Government also developed the National Aged Care Design Principles and Guidelines. The Principles and Guidelines were introduced in July 2024 to support the design of more home‑like, dementia-friendly environments in residential aged care.

The *Designing Culturally Safe Aged Care Homes for Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander People* is a complementary resource to the Principles and Guidelines. This resource is particularly important for people living with dementia. Culturally safe, familiar and community-connected environments can support identity, memory and emotional wellbeing.

A more detailed description on the Australian Government’s response to the final report can be found on the Australian Government Department of Health, Disability and Ageing website.

### The National Dementia Action Plan

Released in December 2024, the 10-year National Dementia Action Plan 2024–2034 (the Action Plan), is a joint initiative between the Australian Government and state and territory governments, which sets out 8 high-level actions aimed at improving the lives of people living with dementia and their families and carers, including improved health and wellbeing outcomes.

The 8 actions seek to:

- Promote equity and human rights
- Tackle stigma, improve awareness and promote inclusivity
- Empower individuals and communities to minimise risk where they can, and delay onset and progression
- Improve dementia diagnosis and post-diagnostic care and support
- Improve treatment, coordination and support for people living with dementia
- Support carers of people living with dementia
- Build capability of the workforce to care for and support people living with dementia
- Improve dementia data, maximise the impact of dementia research and promote innovation.

The Action Plan will be implemented through three Collective Priority Frameworks. The framework is a document that helps governments across Australia focus and align their efforts on specific priority actions.

The first Collective Priority Framework 2025 to 2027 identifies 3 priority actions:

- Empower individuals and communities to minimise risk where they can, and delay onset and progression
- Improve dementia diagnosis and post-diagnostic care and support
- Improve dementia data, maximise the impact of dementia research and promote innovation.

A National Dementia Action Plan Implementation Group of government officials and people with lived experience of dementia will oversee implementation.

The Action Plan forms part of the Australian Government’s response to the Royal Commission and contributes to Australia’s international obligations as a member state to the World Health Organization’s Global action plan on the public health response to dementia 2017–2025.

#### Tracking the Action Plan’s progress

Within each of the 8 actions are *measures of progress*, which are broad statements to determine whether the Plan is meeting its objectives. Changes over time in these measures of progress are tracked by the AIHW using specific metrics (or indicators), which is updated annually. Data on the state of play at the start of the Action Plan are available at National Dementia Action Plan indicators dashboard.

In addition to the indicators dashboard, the Australian and state and territory governments provide annual activity reports on activities related to the Action Plan, such as new programs or changes to existing policies and programs. A mid-point review will also be undertaken to comprehensively assess progress against the Action Plan.

For more information about the Action Plan, see the Department of Health, Disability and Ageing website.

### The Aged Care Diversity Framework

In 2017 the Australian Government launched the Aged Care Diversity Framework and associated action plans to address the needs of older people from diverse backgrounds, including those with cognitive impairment and dementia. The Diversity Framework provides a mechanism for government, aged care providers, peak bodies and representative groups, service users, and their families and carers, to ensure diversity is embedded in the design and delivery of aged care services.

In February 2019, four Action Plans designed to address the specific needs and challenges faced by diverse groups in the community were launched under the Aged Care Diversity Framework for:

- First Nations people
- older people from Culturally and Linguistically Diverse Communities
- people who identify as lesbian, gay, bisexual, trans/transgender, intersex, and other sexuality (including asexual), gender, and bodily diverse people (LGBTIQ+)
- actions common to all diverse older people, in recognition of the many commonalities within and between diverse groups.

These plans assist aged care providers to identify actions they could take to deliver more inclusive and culturally appropriate services for their residents and clients. They acknowledge that there is no ‘one-size-fits-all’ approach to diversity, and that each aged care provider will be starting from a different place and operating in a different context.

### Dementia research

Australian dementia researchers funded by the Australian Government through the National Health and Medical Research Council (NHMRC) and Medical Research Future Fund have a renowned reputation for producing international-leading work. Australian research has high potential to positively impact people with dementia, particularly when research is collaborative and conducted on a large-scale (Moira Clay Consulting 2020).

The Australian Government invested $200 million over five years from 2014 to 2019 through the Boosting Dementia Research Initiative (BDRI) to accelerate research, enhance collaboration and promote advances in dementia research and treatment. The BDRI initiative included the establishment of the NHMRC National Institute for Dementia Research (NNIDR) (which operated until 30 June 2020) to target, coordinate and translate the strategic expansion of dementia research in Australia under the BDRI.

Funding from the BDRI supported the development of the Australian Dementia Network Registry, Australia’s first dementia clinical quality registry to collect data to monitor, report and improve clinical care for people with dementia.

The NHMRC continues to support investigator-led and priority driven dementia research through NHMRC’s grant program schemes.

The Medical Research Future Fund is a $25.4 billion long-term investment that aims to support health and medical research and innovation. The Dementia, Ageing and Aged Care Mission (the Mission) is investing $185 million over 10 years under the Medical Research Future Fund to improve outcomes for people living with dementia and older people. The Mission will support older people to maintain their health and quality of life as they age, live independently for longer, and access quality care when they need it. As of June 2026, the Mission has awarded 86 grants to the value of $147.2 million. Of these grants, 36 are focused on dementia research with a total value of $70.65 million.

Grant funding for dementia research is also provided through other Medical Research Future Fund research initiatives in addition to the Dementia Ageing and Aged Care Mission.

The government also funds a broad range of research and innovation projects on emerging priorities in dementia. The government funded the establishment of Aged Care Research and Industry Innovation Australia (ARIIA) to equip the aged care sector with knowledge and skills to implement evidence-based practices and new technologies. ARIIA focus on the use of innovation to improve workforce capability, including the use of technology to support care recipients to maintain independence for longer.

### The AIHW National Centre for Monitoring Dementia

There are still persistent gaps in national data that limit monitoring and reporting on dementia in Australia and planning for dementia programs and services. The AIHW National Centre for Monitoring Dementia, was established in 2021 and is undertaking routine monitoring of dementia in Australia, addressing existing data gaps and informing specific Government policy on the needs for people living with dementia. The National Centre for Monitoring Dementia currently undertakes activities in two main streams:

- Analysis and reporting: including routine monitoring of dementia via the Dementia in Australia report (this report) and special interest or thematic projects that utilise currently available data to address specific policy and research information priorities and dementia data gaps.
- Improving dementia data: including projects to address dementia data gaps or data quality issues.

For more information on current dementia data gaps, and ways in which these gaps can be systematically and strategically addressed, see the National Dementia Data Improvement Plan 2023–2034.

## Technical notes

Data presented in this report are sourced from a range of data collections from several organisations, including:

- Australian Institute of Health and Welfare (AIHW)
- Australian Bureau of Statistics (ABS)
- Australian Government Department of Health, Disability and Ageing
- Dementia Australia
- Dementia Support Australia.

For a summary and detailed description of each data source, refer to Data sources.

The latest data have been used wherever possible. Data are subject to change due to the nature and timing of the data collection, ongoing updates and maintenance and identification of errors or anomalies. Revisions and changes in coverage should be considered when interpreting changes over time.

For information on the methodologies used to derive estimates (for example, dementia prevalence or aged care expenditure) and other statistics presented in this report, see Methods. Differences between data collections and methodological approaches may influence the comparability of estimates across data sources within this report, as well as with other published information not estimated by AIHW. Where relevant, these considerations have been noted within the report.

Numbers are generally rounded to the nearest 10, 100 or 1,000 in text. Percentages and rates are generally rounded to whole numbers, except for numbers less than 10 which are rounded to 1 decimal place. For unrounded numbers, refer to the Supplementary data tables.

For definitions of technical concepts used throughout this report, see Glossary.

The report uses the Australian Burden of Disease Study (ABDS) for 2018 and 2024:

- The 2024 ABDS provides projected estimates of burden of disease for 220 diseases and injuries for the total population in 2024, with comparisons over time for 2011, 2015, 2018 and 2023. The study used trend analysis techniques to project changes in sex- and age-specific rates in disease burden. The 2024 ABDS also provides estimates of the disease burden attributable to specific risk factors. The ABDS uses Australian data sources where available and adapts the methods from global studies to produce estimates that are relevant to the Australian context (AIHW 2024). Full analysis techniques can be found in the ABDS technical notes.
- The 2018 ABDS study provides the latest data on subnational burden of disease estimates, including by state and territory, remoteness and socioeconomic group, including comparisons over time (2011 and 2015) (AIHW 2021).
- The 2018 ABDS was also used for First Nations-specific burden of disease estimates for the First Nations population for 2018 and comparisons with 2011 (AIHW 2022). The First Nations Burden of Disease Study 2022 was published in March 2026 and will be incorporated into the next release of the Dementia in Australia report.

For dementia, the fatal burden estimates were derived from the AIHW National Mortality Database and considered high quality. National non-fatal burden estimates for dementia were based on prevalence rates published in this online report applied to the relevant Australian estimated resident populations. The severity distribution was based on estimates published by Barendregt and Bonneux (1998) for those aged under 80 and from a study by Lucca et al. (2015) for those aged 80 and over. The quality of the non-fatal burden estimates could be improved if more recent and more generalisable data on dementia prevalence and severity in Australia becomes available.

A comparative risk assessment method was used to quantify the impact of each risk factor on the disease burden for associated diseases, referred to as the ‘attributable burden’. Diseases that were found to have a causal association with dementia and their associated relative risks (the amount of additional risk of developing dementia if exposed to the risk factor) were based on those used in recent Global Burden of Disease studies and several epidemiological studies. The prevalence of exposure to each risk factor was derived from a variety of Australian-specific data sources.
